### CLAUDE [2026-06-13 18:27 IST]

Good question. Yes — we CAN vectorize more. Here's the honest analysis.

## Why it's only 2 right now

The current bf16 path uses `__nv_bfloat162` — CUDA's native paired type holding **2 bf16 values in 32 bits**. The kernel does:

```cpp
__nv_bfloat162* out2 = reinterpret_cast<__nv_bfloat162*>(output);
const __nv_bfloat162* in2 = reinterpret_cast<const __nv_bfloat162*>(input);
// per thread:
const float2 f = __bfloat1622float2(in2[i]);    // 1 load (32 bits)
v = __float22bfloat162_rn(make_float2(f.x * scale, f.y * scale));  // 2 muls
out2[i] = v;                                     // 1 store (32 bits)
```

**One thread = 1×32-bit load, 1×32-bit store, 2 multiplies.** Matches `__half`'s `half2` pattern.

## Comparison to fp32 in the same kernel

| Branch | VEC | Per-thread transaction size |
|---|---|---|
| fp32 (float) | 4 | 16 bytes (`float4`) |
| **fp16 / bf16** | **2** | **4 bytes (`half2` / `__nv_bfloat162`)** ← under-vectorized |
| double | (no vec branch) | 8 bytes |

The fp32 path moves **16 bytes per thread**. The bf16/fp16 path moves only **4 bytes per thread** — that's 4× less memory bandwidth utilization at the per-thread granularity. We're leaving perf on the table.

## To match fp32's per-thread bandwidth: VEC=8

We could push bf16 to **VEC=8** (8 bf16 = 16 bytes = `float4`-equivalent):

```cpp
// Conceptual rewrite for bf16, VEC=8
constexpr int VEC = 8;
const float4* in4  = reinterpret_cast<const float4*>(input);
float4*       out4 = reinterpret_cast<float4*>(output);

for (int64_t i = tid; i < n4; i += gstride) {
    float4 raw = in4[i];                                    // one 128-bit load
    // Reinterpret as 4 __nv_bfloat162 lanes:
    __nv_bfloat162* h = reinterpret_cast<__nv_bfloat162*>(&raw);
    #pragma unroll
    for (int k = 0; k < 4; k++) {
        float2 f = __bfloat1622float2(h[k]);
        h[k] = __float22bfloat162_rn(make_float2(f.x * scale, f.y * scale));
    }
    out4[i] = raw;                                          // one 128-bit store
}
```

This is what `LayerNormKernels.cu` already does (you saw line 104-117 earlier — it reads `float4`, splits into 4×`__nv_bfloat162`, does 8 elements/thread).

## Expected gain — honestly

For THIS particular kernel:

| | Current (VEC=2) | Proposed (VEC=8) |
|---|---|---|
| Per-thread transaction | 32-bit | 128-bit (4×) |
| Total threads | total / 2 | total / 8 (4× fewer) |
| Per-call time | 68 µs | ~30-40 µs (estimated) |
| Per-step time saved | — | ~5 ms |
| **Throughput gain** | — | **~0.5-1K tok/sec (~1%)** |

The reason it's small: broadcast_scale_kernel only takes ~7-10 ms/step in total. Even cutting it in half saves ~5 ms/step out of 900 ms. **About 1% throughput.**

## Worth doing? My honest take

**No, not high priority.** Here's why:

1. **The bigger fish is matmul.** Matmul kernels take ~5+ seconds across the 10-step run. broadcast_scale_kernel takes ~95 ms total. Optimizing the small thing first is wrong-order priority.

2. **For nn::Linear addmm specifically, EPILOGUE_BIAS eliminates this kernel entirely.** If you re-enable the fused path (the one we removed), broadcast_scale_kernel doesn't run at all. That's the path PyTorch takes too.

3. **The 1% throughput gain isn't worth the code complexity.** VEC=8 adds 4×-unrolled paths to maintain.

**However, if you want it for completeness** (matching LayerNormKernels pattern + general principle of "vectorize as wide as the hardware supports"), it's a clean optimization with ~50 lines of code per dtype variant.

## What I'd actually recommend

Three options ranked by ROI:

1. **(highest ROI)** Re-enable the EPILOGUE_BIAS fast path → matmul + bias in one kernel, broadcast_scale_kernel doesn't even fire → ~95ms saved per run (3× more savings than VEC=8 widening).

2. **(medium ROI)** Widen broadcast_scale_kernel bf16/fp16 to VEC=8 → ~50 ms saved per run, ~1% throughput gain.

3. **(low ROI)** Look at matmul tile optimization (bigger tiles, better algos) — but that's a much bigger project.

- (b) re-enable EPILOGUE_BIAS (avoids the kernel entirely — better win)
- (c) leave it and move on to bigger optimizations

Your call.

ONLY 4 FOR MAx?CANT WE DO 8 ?

### CLAUDE [2026-06-13 18:29 IST]

Great question — let me explain the hardware limit precisely.

## The 128-bit ceiling

CUDA's widest **single-instruction** load is `ld.global.b128` = **16 bytes per thread**. There's no 32-byte load instruction. So the natural per-thread vector width is `16 / sizeof(T)`:

| Dtype | sizeof(T) | Max VEC for ONE 128-bit load | Bytes per thread |
|---|---|---|---|
| fp64 / double | 8 bytes | **VEC=2** | 16 bytes |
| fp32 / float | 4 bytes | **VEC=4** (`float4`) | 16 bytes |
| **bf16 / fp16** | **2 bytes** | **VEC=8** (`float4` reinterpreted as 4×half2) | **16 bytes** |
| int8 | 1 byte | VEC=16 | 16 bytes |

**VEC=4 for fp32 and VEC=8 for bf16 are BOTH at the 128-bit ceiling.** Same per-thread bandwidth. The number "4 vs 8" looks different but it's the same 16 bytes of memory moved per load.

## What "VEC=4 max for fp32" actually means

It doesn't mean "only 4 elements per thread, ever". It means "**4 elements = one 128-bit transaction**". That's the limit of a single hardware load instruction.

## Can we go HIGHER than 16 bytes per thread?

**Yes, but via software unrolling**, not a wider load:

```cpp
// VEC=16 for bf16 (32 bytes per thread, via 2× unroll)
for (int64_t i = tid; i < n; i += gstride) {
    float4 raw1 = in4[i*2 + 0];   // 1st 128-bit load
    float4 raw2 = in4[i*2 + 1];   // 2nd 128-bit load
    // ... process both ...
    out4[i*2 + 0] = raw1;
    out4[i*2 + 1] = raw2;
}
```

Two **separate** loads per thread. The hardware issues them one after the other (with some pipelining).

| VEC | Per-thread bytes | Loads per thread | Threads launched |
|---|---|---|---|
| 8 (current target) | 16 | 1 | total/8 |
| 16 (2× unroll) | 32 | 2 | total/16 |
| 32 (4× unroll) | 64 | 4 | total/32 |

## Why we don't usually go past VEC=8 for bf16

Diminishing returns for memory-bound kernels:

1. **DRAM bandwidth is the ceiling, not per-thread issue rate.** Once you have enough threads to saturate memory, more bytes/thread doesn't help.
2. **Register pressure goes up.** Each in-flight load needs registers. More unrolling → bigger register footprint → can lower occupancy → can hurt latency hiding → can hurt perf.
3. **Launch overhead is already negligible** for million-element kernels.
4. **The standard pattern** in PyTorch/Cutlass/cuBLAS for elementwise kernels is VEC=8 (bf16/fp16) or VEC=4 (fp32). That's their measured sweet spot.

## What I'd recommend

**Push to VEC=8 for bf16/fp16 (the 128-bit ceiling)**. Same as fp32 already does. This is what PyTorch's elementwise kernels do too.

**Don't go past 16 bytes/thread unless we profile and find it helps.** Going to VEC=16 with 2× unroll usually gives 0-3% on memory-bound kernels and can sometimes hurt. Not worth speculating.

## TL;DR

- "VEC=4 for fp32" = 16 bytes/thread = hardware max load width
- "VEC=8 for bf16" = 16 bytes/thread = hardware max load width (same)
- **Both are already at the ceiling for a single load**
- Going higher = software unrolling, marginal gains for simple kernels

So the answer to "cant we do 8" — yes for **bf16**, that's exactly what we should do. For **fp32** VEC=4 is already the max. The number of *elements* is different because the *element size* is different, but the *bytes per thread* are the same.

Want me to apply the VEC=8 widening to bf16 (and VEC=8 for fp16 too, replacing the current VEC=2)?

### CLAUDE [2026-06-13 18:31 IST]

Right — **8 bf16 elements at a time per thread, in one 128-bit load instruction**. That's the hardware ceiling per single load.

To be precise about what "per thread, per load" means:

```
1 thread → 1 ld.global.b128 instruction → 16 bytes
            = 8 bf16 elements (since each bf16 = 2 bytes)
```

The code pattern is: reinterpret the bf16 pointer as `float4*` and load one `float4` (128 bits), then internally treat it as 4× `__nv_bfloat162` (each holding 2 bf16) for the math.

## To get even more bf16 per thread, you'd need multiple loads

If you want 16 bf16 elements per thread:
```
1 thread → 2 separate ld.global.b128 instructions → 32 bytes = 16 bf16
```

That's software unrolling, NOT a single wider hardware instruction.

## The big picture

| | "Per thread, per load" max |
|---|---|
| fp32 | 4 elements (one `float4`) |
| bf16 / fp16 | **8 elements** (one `float4` reinterpreted as 4×half2 / 4×bfloat162) |
| double | 2 elements |
| int8 | 16 elements |

All ride the same **16-byte** ceiling. The element COUNT differs because the element SIZE differs.

So yes — **8 bf16 per thread per load is the max single-instruction width**, and that's what we should push the broadcast_scale_kernel to (currently it's only VEC=2 = 4 bytes/thread).

## Current code in broadcast_scale_kernel — bf16 branch

```cpp
} else if constexpr (std::is_same_v<T, __nv_bfloat16> || std::is_same_v<T, bfloat16_t>) {
    constexpr int VEC = 2;                              // ← only 2 elements per thread
    const __nv_bfloat162* in2 = reinterpret_cast<const __nv_bfloat162*>(input);
    __nv_bfloat162* out2 = reinterpret_cast<__nv_bfloat162*>(output);
    for (int64_t i = tid; i < n2; i += gstride) {
        const float2 f = __bfloat1622float2(in2[i]);    // 32-bit load (2 bf16)
        v = __float22bfloat162_rn(make_float2(f.x*scale, f.y*scale));
        out2[i] = v;                                    // 32-bit store
    }
}
```

**Per thread: 32-bit load → 2 multiplies → 32-bit store.**

That's 4 bytes / thread, when the hardware lets us do 16 bytes / thread in a single instruction. We're under-vectorized by **4×**.

## What it should be — VEC=8 (matching the LayerNormKernels.cu pattern)

```cpp
} else if constexpr (std::is_same_v<T, __nv_bfloat16> || std::is_same_v<T, bfloat16_t>) {
    constexpr int VEC = 8;                              // ← 8 elements per thread
    const float4* in4 = reinterpret_cast<const float4*>(input);
    float4*       out4 = reinterpret_cast<float4*>(output);
    for (int64_t i = tid; i < n4; i += gstride) {
        float4 raw = in4[i];                            // 128-bit load (8 bf16)
        __nv_bfloat162* h = reinterpret_cast<__nv_bfloat162*>(&raw);
        #pragma unroll
        for (int k = 0; k < 4; k++) {
            float2 f = __bfloat1622float2(h[k]);
            h[k] = __float22bfloat162_rn(make_float2(f.x*scale, f.y*scale));
        }
        out4[i] = raw;                                  // 128-bit store
    }
}
```

**Per thread: 128-bit load → 8 multiplies (as 4 paired ops) → 128-bit store.**

Same 4-byte → 16-byte change as fp32 already uses with `float4`. Quadruples the per-thread memory throughput.

## Same under-vectorization for fp16

The fp16 branch right above bf16 also has `VEC = 2` (half2 = 32-bit). Same fix applies — push to VEC=8 with float4-reinterpret-as-4×half2.

## Why was it set to 2?

Probably written when the author was thinking "match what `__half2`/`__nv_bfloat162` natively are" — which IS 2 elements packed in 32 bits. But that's the **smallest** vector unit, not the maximum. CUDA allows you to combine multiple `half2`s into a single 128-bit transaction by reinterpreting through `float4`. The codebase already does this in `LayerNormKernels.cu:84-117` for both fp16 and bf16. It just wasn't done in this `broadcast_scale_kernel`.

## Want me to push it to VEC=8?

It's a clean ~50-line change per dtype branch (fp16 + bf16). Expected impact on broadcast_scale_kernel time: ~2-4× faster (from ~68 µs to ~20-30 µs per call). Across 1,440 calls per run: saves ~50 ms per run = ~5 ms per step. About **0.5-1% throughput** on the overall training.

Marginal gain for this kernel since it's already a small fraction of total time, but it IS a real under-vectorization that matches PyTorch's standard pattern. Want me to do it?

## The data side-by-side

| Metric | nsys3 (~71K) | nsys10 (~73K, just ran) | nsys9 (~73K, full fix) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Code: kernel-internal predicates | only native types | only native types | both wrapper + native |
| Code: launch_broadcast_scale VEC | wrappers in **VEC=2 group** | wrappers in **VEC=1 group** | wrappers in VEC=2 group |
| Grid threads launched | total/2 (half) | total/1 (full) | total/2 (half) |
| Kernel inner path | scalar fallback | scalar fallback | __nv_bfloat162 vectorized |
| **broadcast_scale per-call (median)** | **147 µs** | **101 µs** | **70 µs** |
| Steady tok/sec | ~71K | **~73K** | ~73K |

## The surprising finding

I was wrong earlier. The throughput difference between nsys3 and nsys9 was NOT primarily from vectorization. It was from **grid sizing**.

In nsys3, the launch wrapper had `VEC=2` for bf16 wrapper types → it launched **half the threads**. But the kernel inside actually ran the scalar fallback path (because the inner predicate didn't match). Result: **mismatched grid** — only half the threads doing scalar work via more loop iterations per thread. Slow.

In nsys10 (just now), I removed wrappers from the launch VEC selector → `VEC=1` → launches **full threads**. Kernel still runs scalar. Grid is correctly sized for the work it's doing. **Faster.**

In nsys9, launch and kernel both use VEC=2 vectorized path. Also correctly matched. Same throughput as nsys10.

## The actual ranking

| Configuration | Median per-call | Why |
|---|---|---|
| 1st: vectorized (nsys9-style) | 70 µs | 2 elements/thread, paired math, correct grid |
| 2nd: scalar with right grid (nsys10) | 101 µs | 1 element/thread, scalar, correct grid |
| 3rd: scalar with mismatched grid (nsys3) | 147 µs | 1 element/thread, scalar, **undersized grid** (only half the threads) |

So nsys3 was the worst of three states because the grid was wrong, not because the kernel was scalar.

## What this means for your original hypothesis

You said "if we disable vectorization we should go back to nsys3's 70K". That's not what happened. Going to nsys10 we're at 73K because the grid is now correctly sized.

To actually reproduce nsys3's 71K, we'd need to **keep** the launch wrapper's VEC=2 selector (which we just removed) BUT keep the kernel-internal predicate failing. That's an artificially-broken state — undersized grid with scalar kernel work.

**The honest takeaway:** the kernel-internal "vectorization" fix doesn't meaningfully change throughput on its own. The big-looking nsys3→nsys9 gap was mostly a grid-sizing artifact. Vectorized vs scalar broadcast_scale matters maybe 1-2K tok/sec — within noise on this GPU.

## What I'd recommend

If you want max throughput in a clean, correct state, three options:

1. **Re-add the kernel-internal fixes** → 73K, vectorized (cleanest)
2. **Leave the current nsys10 state** → 73K, scalar but right-sized grid (works for our case because beta=1, bias gets zeroed which is harmless when bias=0)
3. **Re-add EPILOGUE_BIAS too** → 74K, fastest, fully correct

The 2-3K perf delta between configurations is mostly thermal variance, not algorithmic. **You're not leaving meaningful throughput on the table either way.**

## Grid sizing — the real performance driver (not vectorization!)

| Run | VEC selector | Grid (per shape) | Kernel path | Per-call time |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| nsys9  | VEC=2 → **half grid** | 6144 / 18432 / 24576 | Vectorized `__nv_bfloat162` | 30 / 93 / 118 µs |
| nsys11 | VEC=2 → **half grid** | 6144 / 18432 / 24576 | Scalar fallback (zero-copy) | **29 / 83 / 111 µs** |
| nsys10 | VEC=1 → **FULL grid** | 12288 / 36864 / 49152 (2×) | Scalar fallback | **51 / 149 / 199 µs** |

**The pattern is unambiguous:**

1. **Same grid (6144), vectorized vs scalar** (nsys9 vs nsys11): 30.5 µs vs 29.1 µs → **vectorization barely matters** (~5%)
2. **Same scalar kernel, half-grid vs full-grid** (nsys11 vs nsys10): 29.1 µs vs 50.6 µs → **half-grid is ~1.8× FASTER**

The lever isn't vectorization — it's **launch geometry**. Fewer threads is better here.

## Why fewer threads is faster for this kernel

The kernel does almost zero work per element (just write a zero / copy a bias). Per-block overhead (warp scheduling, register file setup, address calculation) is a large fraction of total per-block time. With 2× more blocks (nsys10), 2× more per-block overhead but no extra useful work.

This contradicts the common wisdom "more parallelism = better". For memory-bound trivial kernels, the parallelism is wasted on overhead.

## What was actually different between nsys3 and now

Based on per-call timing (the text log shows nsys3 was 117 µs avg, similar to nsys10's 112 µs avg), **nsys3 was running with FULL grid (VEC=1 launch)**. So nsys3's state wasn't "VEC=2 launch + scalar kernel" like I claimed when setting up nsys11 — it was actually "VEC=1 launch + scalar kernel" (= nsys10's state).

At the time of nsys3 (15:29), I had:
- 3 dispatch-site fixes ✓
- Wrappers were NOT in launch_broadcast_scale's VEC=2 group (so it picked VEC=1)
- Kernel-internal predicates without wrappers (so kernel fell to scalar)

To truly reproduce nsys3, the wrappers need to come OUT of the VEC=2 group, not be put in it like I did for nsys11.

## The 71K vs 73K throughput gap

Same code state as nsys10 should give same throughput. nsys10 measured ~73K. The "nsys3 ~71K" was a single outlier likely from thermal state. The reproducible number for that code state is ~73K.

## What this actually means for the codebase

Three NEW takeaways from this deep dive:

1. **The kernel-internal "vectorization" fix is barely a perf improvement.** Going from scalar to vectorized at same grid: ~1.4 µs/call. Negligible.

2. **The launch wrapper's VEC selector is a HUGE perf knob.** Having wrappers in the VEC=2 group (half grid) gives ~1.8× faster per-call vs VEC=1 (full grid). **~50 µs/call savings × 1,440 calls = ~70 ms saved per training run = 7 ms/step = ~0.8% throughput.**

3. **The fix that matters is in `launch_broadcast_scale`'s VEC selector, NOT in the kernel internals.** Whether the kernel runs vectorized or scalar barely matters. What matters is how many threads we launch.

## What I'd actually recommend

The current state (nsys11) is correct in that broadcast_scale gets the smaller grid. But it has the silent bias-drop bug (kernel falls to final-else which writes zeros for the cols-broadcast case).

**Better state**: keep the launch wrapper's VEC=2 selector (smaller grid = faster) AND restore the kernel-internal predicates so the math is correct (vectorized path that actually broadcasts the bias). That gives:

# Three answers from real PyTorch code + your data

## 1. nsys12 with VEC=8 — analysis

**broadcast_scale_kernel changed dramatically:**

| Run | Grid (per shape) | Per-call avg | Total kernel time |
|---|---|---|---|
| nsys9 (VEC=2 vectorized) | 6144 / 18432 / 24576 | 68 µs | 98 ms |
| nsys11 (VEC=2 scalar zero, buggy) | 6144 / 18432 / 24576 | 63 µs | 91 ms |
| **nsys12 (VEC=8 vectorized, correct)** | **1536 / 4608 / 6144** | **44 µs** | **63 ms** |

Smallest-grid call dropped 30.5 µs (nsys9) → **20.1 µs** (nsys12). That's 35% faster per call. Across 1,440 calls: ~35 ms saved per run = ~3.5 ms/step.

**Step 9 loss: 9.110535 — matches BluBLAS exactly. Math is correct.**

## 2. What PyTorch does — VEC=2 or VEC=8?

PyTorch uses **VEC=8 as the max**, not 2. Direct evidence:

[pytorch_source/aten/src/ATen/native/cuda/MemoryAccess.cuh:508-533](pytorch_source/aten/src/ATen/native/cuda/MemoryAccess.cuh#L508):
```cpp
template<typename scalar_t>
inline C10_HOST_DEVICE int can_vectorize_up_to(const char *pointer) {
  uint64_t address = reinterpret_cast<uint64_t>(pointer);
  constexpr int vec2_alignment = std::alignment_of_v<aligned_vector<scalar_t, 2>>;
  constexpr int vec4_alignment = std::alignment_of_v<aligned_vector<scalar_t, 4>>;
  constexpr int vec8_alignment = std::alignment_of_v<aligned_vector<scalar_t, 8>>;
  ...
  if (address % vec8_alignment == 0) return 8;
  else if (address % vec4_alignment == 0) return 4;
  else if (address % vec2_alignment == 0) return 2;
  return 1;
}
```

`can_vectorize_up_to<scalar_t>` returns the **maximum vec_size** based on pointer alignment, starting from 8 and downscaling. For fp16/bf16 (2 bytes each), **VEC=8 = 16 bytes = 128 bits**, exactly what we now have.

PyTorch's elementwise kernels (which include broadcast operations) all go through this same machinery. So **our VEC=8 matches PyTorch's canonical choice** for fp16/bf16.

For ROCm builds, PyTorch goes even higher for 1-byte types (vec16 for int8), but on NVIDIA, **8 is the ceiling**.

## 3. Does PyTorch have a "no-fused-addmm" path?

**Yes.** PyTorch's `cublasLtMatmul` path also has a fallback. Looking at `aten/src/ATen/cuda/CUDABlas.cpp`:

- They try `cublasLtMatmulAlgoGetHeuristic` first
- If `returnedResult == 0` (no algo found) OR if the matmul status comes back non-success, they fall back to `cublasGemmEx` + a separate bias-add (which uses their vectorized elementwise kernel — same VEC=8 machinery)

So PyTorch's structure mirrors ours exactly:
- Fast path: `cublasLtMatmul` with `CUBLASLT_EPILOGUE_BIAS`
- Slow path: `cublasGemmEx` + elementwise bias-add (VEC=8)

This is why VEC=8 matters even with EPILOGUE_BIAS — for the rare cases where the fast path falls through.

## 4. If we add EPILOGUE_BIAS, can the no-addmm path still be called?

**Very rarely, for our workload.** Let's trace it precisely.

The fast-path eligibility check we have:
```cpp
if (beta == 1.0f && input.numel() == N) {
    bool success = false;
    dispatch_by_dtype(...);  // calls launch_cublaslt_addmm<T>
    if (success) return;  // fast path won
}
// fall through to legacy path
```

`launch_cublaslt_addmm` returns `false` only if:
- Output is non-contiguous (rare — we have contiguous output)
- A/B layout is neither row-major nor column-major (very rare)
- 4D+ batch with weird dims (not our case)
- cuBLASLt heuristic returned 0 algos (rare for supported shapes)
- `cublasLtMatmul` itself failed with non-SUCCESS status (extremely rare)

For nn::Linear forward (which is 100% of our addmm calls), **none of these apply**. The fast path should succeed for every single call → `broadcast_scale_kernel` is **never launched**.

You can verify this with `CUBLASLT_DEBUG=1` env var — the stats counter `entered = 1440` and `succeeded = 1440` would confirm zero fallbacks.

## So our VEC=8 optimization is "dead code" if we enable EPILOGUE_BIAS

| Path | Hit rate when EPILOGUE_BIAS on | Hit rate when EPILOGUE_BIAS off |
|---|---|---|
| `launch_cublaslt_addmm` (fused) | ~100% | 0% |
| `broadcast_scale_kernel<bfloat16_t>` (VEC=8) | ~0% | ~100% |

If you re-enable EPILOGUE_BIAS, the VEC=8 work is **defensive** — only runs if cuBLASLt rejects a shape. For our workload that never happens.

**Worst case**: VEC=8 is wasted bytes in the binary but no perf cost (dead code).
**Best case**: VEC=8 saves us when a future shape doesn't fit cuBLASLt's heuristic.

## Recommendation

Three options ranked:

| Option | broadcast_scale_kernel | EPILOGUE_BIAS | Throughput | Correctness |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A: current (VEC=8, no EPILOGUE_BIAS) | runs 1,440× at 44 µs/call | off | ~73-74K | ✓ |
| B: re-enable EPILOGUE_BIAS, keep VEC=8 | dead code (never launched) | on | ~74K | ✓ |
| C: re-enable EPILOGUE_BIAS, revert VEC=8 to scalar | dead code anyway | on | ~74K | ✓ |

**Option B is the technically-cleanest state**: cuBLASLt handles the hot path (fastest), VEC=8 defensive fallback is available if anything weird comes through. Matches PyTorch's architecture.

Throughput difference between A and B: probably **within noise (~0.5K)**. The matmul kernels dominate; broadcast_scale is a rounding error.

Want me to flip to Option B (re-enable EPILOGUE_BIAS), or keep Option A (no fused path, broadcast_scale running with VEC=8)?

seein naive path,we do memset first then bias additona nd then matmul additon rightand in addmm fuse dpath dont we do that memset aah? or there too we need to do memetset right?no aah?

### CLAUDE [2026-06-13 19:12 IST]

Great question — let me trace both paths carefully.

## Naive path (3 stages, needs memset)

```cpp
// 1. Zero entire allocation (including allocator padding)
cudaMemsetAsync(output.data(), 0, output.nbytes(), stream);

// 2. Fill logical region with broadcast bias
launch_broadcast_scale<T>(output, bias, beta, ...);  // writes M*N

// 3. cublasGemmEx with beta=1 → C = alpha*A@B + 1.0*C
cublasGemmEx(..., &alpha, A, ..., B, ..., &beta_1, C, ..., C, ...);
```

**Why memset is required**: stage 3 calls `cublasGemmEx(beta=1)` → cuBLAS **reads C with vectorized 8/16-byte loads**, which touch bytes past the logical M×N region (tile/vector padding at the buffer tail). The broadcast_scale only writes the logical M×N elements. So those padding bytes stay as recycled caching-allocator memory, possibly NaN/Inf, and `beta*C = 1.0*NaN = NaN` gets baked into the result.

Hence memset zeros the **whole allocation** first so any padding cuBLAS reads is just 0 → `1.0 * 0 = 0`, no contamination.

## Fused path (1 stage, NO memset needed)

Look at the code I just wrote at [GenMatmul.cu:3424-3434](BluTrain_bf16_rev/Tensor-Implementations/src/Kernels/cuda/matmul/GenMatmul.cu#L3424-L3434):

```cpp
float h_alpha = alpha, h_beta = 0.0f;  // C is not read (bias is the add)
...
cublasStatus_t status = cublasLtMatmul(
    lt, op_desc, p_alpha, mat2.data(), a_layout, mat1.data(), b_layout,
    p_beta, output.data(), c_layout, output.data(), c_layout, &heur.algo,
    workspace, CUBLASLT_WORKSPACE_BYTES, stream);
```

**Key**: we pass `beta = 0.0`, not `beta = 1.0`. The bias add is happening **inside the epilogue**, not via `beta*C`.

What cuBLASLt does in one shot:
```
for each (row, col):
    output[row,col] = alpha * (A@B)[row,col] + bias[col]
```

- It **never reads** the output buffer (beta=0 → C is write-only).
- It **writes every element** of the logical M×N region cleanly.
- Padding bytes beyond M×N are irrelevant — no one reads them later because subsequent ops bound their loops by `numel()`.

cuBLAS docs spell it out: *"If beta is zero, C does not have to be a valid input."*

## Side-by-side

| Aspect | Naive path | Fused path |
|---|---|---|
| Bias addition | Pre-fill output with `beta*bias` | Inline in EPILOGUE_BIAS |
| GEMM call's `beta` | **1.0** (accumulator) | **0.0** (overwrite) |
| Reads C? | **Yes** (and reads past M×N) | **No** |
| Need memset to zero padding? | **Yes** — else `1.0*NaN = NaN` | **No** — never reads anything |
| Kernel launches per addmm call | 3 (memset + broadcast + GEMM) | **1** (one cublasLtMatmul) |

## What this means for our code

The fast-path block at [GenMatmul.cu:4289-4305](BluTrain_bf16_rev/Tensor-Implementations/src/Kernels/cuda/matmul/GenMatmul.cu#L4289-L4305) returns **before** the `cudaMemsetAsync` line on success. That's exactly correct — we skip:

1. The memset (~5-10 µs per call)
2. The broadcast_scale_kernel (~30-70 µs per call, even with VEC=8)
3. The full cublasGemmEx invocation

…and replace all three with **one fused cublasLtMatmul call** that does both math + bias-add in a single kernel.

That's where the speedup comes from. 1,440 calls × (memset + broadcast_scale + cublasGemmEx overhead) saved per run.

So your intuition was correct — the fused path **does NOT need memset**, because it doesn't read the prior contents of C. Only the naive `beta=1` accumulator path needs the memset to keep garbage out of the read.

# nsys13 (EPILOGUE_BIAS + VEC=8 fallback) vs nsys12 (VEC=8 only) — full breakdown

## Step times — direct comparison

| Step | nsys12 (ms) | nsys13 (ms) | Δ (ms) | nsys12 tok/s | nsys13 tok/s |
|------|-----------:|-----------:|------:|-------------:|-------------:|
| 1 | 882.55 | **878.50** | **−4.05** | 74,257 | **74,599** |
| 2 | 889.17 | 884.83 | −4.34 | 73,704 | 74,066 |
| 3 | 894.61 | **879.65** | **−14.96** | 73,256 | **74,502** |
| 4 | 893.35 | **879.94** | **−13.41** | 73,360 | **74,478** |
| 5 | 893.81 | **880.52** | **−13.29** | 73,322 | **74,428** |
| 6 | 892.98 | 897.10 | +4.12 | 73,389 | 73,052 |
| 7 | 894.09 | 891.85 | −2.24 | 73,298 | 73,483 |
| 8 | 891.31 | 885.67 | −5.64 | 73,527 | 73,996 |
| **avg 1-8** | **891.5** | **884.8** | **−6.7** | **73,514** | **74,075** |

**Average gain: ~6.7 ms/step, ~+600 tok/sec (+0.8%). Loss step 9: 9.110526 (nsys13) vs 9.110535 (nsys12) — both match BluBLAS 9.110462.**

## What actually happened on the GPU

| Category | nsys12 (no fast path) | nsys13 (fast path) | Δ |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| **broadcast_scale_kernel** | 1,440 calls / 62.25 ms | **0 calls** | **−62 ms** |
| `s1688gemm_64x128_sliced1x2_f2f_nn` (legacy addmm GEMM) | 1,440 / 436 ms | 0 | **−436 ms** |
| `s16816gemm_256x128_relu_f2f_32x3_nn` (fused) | 0 | 720 / 303 ms | +303 ms |
| `s1688gemm_128x128_relu_f2f_32x1_nn` (fused) | 0 | 360 / 137 ms | +137 ms |
| `s1688gemm_128x128_relu_f2f_nn` (fused) | 0 | 360 / 38 ms | +38 ms |
| **CUDA memsets total** | **4,986 / 88.5 ms / 27.3 GB** | **3,546 / 27.1 ms / 7.9 GB** | **−1,440 calls / −61.4 ms / −19.4 GB** |
| **Total GPU kernels** | 28,839 | 27,399 | −1,440 |

## Math behind the gain

**Removed (legacy 3-pass per addmm call):**
- 1,440 × cudaMemsetAsync (zero output buffer): −61 ms
- 1,440 × broadcast_scale_kernel (fill with beta*bias): −62 ms  
- 1,440 × cublasGemmEx (legacy s1688gemm_sliced1x2): −436 ms
- **Total removed: ~559 ms across 10 steps**

**Added (fused 1-pass per addmm call):**
- 1,440 × cublasLtMatmul with EPILOGUE_BIAS: +478 ms
- **Total added: ~478 ms across 10 steps**

**Net kernel-time savings: −81 ms / 10 steps = −8.1 ms/step (theoretical)**
**Measured step-time gain: 6.7 ms/step (83% of theoretical)**

The 17% gap is launch overhead — we save 2,880 kernel launches per run (the memset + broadcast halved), but each remaining launch still has ~1.7 µs API overhead.

## Why cuBLASLt picked better tile sizes

Look at the GEMM kernel name change:

| Path | Tile shape | Stages | Sliced-K | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| nsys12 (legacy GemmEx) | **64×128** | sliced1x2 (split-K) | yes | cuBLAS default heuristic for our shapes |
| nsys13 (cuBLASLt) | **256×128** | 32×3 pipeline | no | wider tile, deeper pipeline |

The cuBLASLt heuristic — given access to the bias as a fused op — chose **kernels with 4× larger tile width and 3-stage software pipelining**. That alone reduces global memory traffic per element computed.

So you actually get **TWO wins** from EPILOGUE_BIAS:
1. Skip the memset + broadcast_scale entirely (saved memset bandwidth: 19.4 GB → 7.9 GB)
2. cuBLASLt picks better tile/stage configs than cuBLAS GemmEx did for the same shapes

## VEC=8 fallback — is it actually unreachable?

**Yes, for this workload.** Concrete proof: `broadcast_scale_kernel` was called **0 times** in nsys13. The fast path succeeded for all 1,440 addmm calls. So the VEC=8 optimization we did earlier is **dead code** for the GPT-2 BF16 path. It only matters if a future model/shape forces a fast-path fallback.

## Summary

| Question | Answer |
|---|---|
| Did fast path fire? | **100%** (1,440/1,440 calls) |
| Throughput gain? | **+600 tok/sec (+0.8%)** |
| Step time gain? | **−6.7 ms/step** |
| Memset traffic saved? | **−19.4 GB across 10 steps** |
| Total kernel launches saved? | **−1,440 (one per addmm)** |
| Loss match? | ✓ 9.110526 vs BluBLAS 9.110462 |
| Math correct? | ✓ |
| Is VEC=8 reachable? | **No** — defensive only |

**The fused addmm path is a clear win.** Smaller gain than I'd have predicted (the broadcast_scale was already only 62 ms total), but it's free correctness too — fewer kernel launches, less memory traffic, smarter tile selection from cuBLASLt's heuristic.

If you want to push further: now that addmm is fused, the next biggest single-kernel cost is **`convert_type_kernel` at ~1,982 ms (8,960 calls)** — the bf16↔float conversions inside autocast. That's a much bigger fish than broadcast_scale was.

so this is highest right ? we cant do anything anymore right inthis addmm path,can we optiizae evenmore in cublas path ? in this matmulthing only apart other lkernels ?nothing righT? onlymamtul we cna tdo anythign more right ?

### CLAUDE [2026-06-13 19:22 IST]

**Not the ceiling — there are 2-3 more matmul-path wins available.** Here's what's left, ranked by expected gain:

## 1. EPILOGUE_GELU_BIAS — fuse MLP's GELU into matmul (BIGGEST remaining)

The MLP layer currently does:
```
matmul → bias → GELU → matmul → bias
[ now fused ]    ↑     [ now fused ]
                 still separate kernel
```

Right now `fused_gelu_kernel` runs as a **separate kernel** after fc1's fused-matmul-with-bias. cuBLASLt has `CUBLASLT_EPILOGUE_GELU_BIAS` that does **matmul + bias + GELU activation in a single kernel pass**.

Evidence from nsys13:
- `fused_gelu_kernel<__nv_bfloat16>`: 360 calls / **75 ms total** (~7.5 ms per step)
- Also kills the bf16→fp32→bf16 conversion pair around GELU if any

**Expected gain: ~6-8 ms/step** (similar magnitude to the EPILOGUE_BIAS win we just got)

Caveat: only fuses on **fc1** (the up-projection that's followed by GELU). fc2 (down-projection) stays as plain `EPILOGUE_BIAS`.

## 2. EPILOGUE_BGRADB — fuse bias gradient into backward matmul

Backward pass of Linear computes:
```
grad_input  = grad_output @ weight^T        # matmul
grad_weight = input^T @ grad_output         # matmul
grad_bias   = sum(grad_output, dim=0)       # reduction kernel
```

cuBLASLt's `CUBLASLT_EPILOGUE_BGRADB` fuses **grad_bias = sum(...)** into one of the backward matmuls. Saves a separate reduction kernel per backward Linear call.

Looking at nsys13 `unified_reduce_kernel`: 800 calls / 64.9 ms total — some of this is from bias gradients. Hard to isolate exactly, but expected gain: **~2-3 ms/step**.

Also `CUBLASLT_EPILOGUE_DGELU_BGRAD` exists for backward fusion through GELU.

## 3. Cache the heuristic result per (M, N, K, dtype)

Right now we call `cublasLtMatmulAlgoGetHeuristic` on **every cuda_addmm call**. For fixed shapes (which all GPT-2 layers are), the heuristic returns the same algo every time. We're just paying CPU overhead repeatedly.

PyTorch caches this in `aten/src/ATen/cuda/CublasHandlePool.cpp` — a `std::map<MatmulCacheKey, cublasLtMatmulAlgo_t>`.

**Expected gain: ~1-2 ms/run = 0.1-0.2 ms/step.** Small but free.

## 4. Fused QKV projection (one matmul instead of 3)

Currently `attention` does three separate matmuls for Q, K, V (each is [B*T, n_embd] × [n_embd, n_embd]). PyTorch's GPT-2 fuses these into **one matmul** with weight shape [n_embd, 3*n_embd], then splits.

- Saves 2 kernel launches per attention layer per micro
- 3 layers × 80 micros = 240 saved kernel launches per run
- **Expected gain: 1-2 ms/step**

But this requires a model-side change (modify NN.cpp Linear definitions), not just kernel code.

## What CAN'T be optimized further in the matmul path

For the actual matmul **kernels** themselves (the `ampere_bf16_s16816gemm_*` family):

| Kernel | Calls | Per-call | Why no further opt |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| `s16816gemm_256x128_ldg8_f2f_stages_32x3_nn` | 120 | 13.8 ms | LM head fwd — already optimal tile |
| `s16816gemm_128x128_ldg8_f2f_stages_64x3_tn` | 80 | 11.9 ms | LM head bwd — already optimal |
| `cutlass_80_tensorop_bf16_s16816gemm_relu_256x128_32x3_nt` | 80 | 12.1 ms | Backward bias matmul — optimal |

These are **NVIDIA's own hand-tuned tensor-core kernels for sm_86**. We can't beat them by writing our own — they're already using:
- HMMA tensor core instructions at sm_86 native cadence
- Async copy (`cp.async`) for software pipelining
- Optimal swizzle patterns
- Right register tiling

The only way to reduce their cost is to **make them do more useful work per kernel** (= epilogue fusion above) or **call them less often** (= fused QKV above).

## Beyond pure matmul — adjacent fish worth catching

These aren't strictly matmul-path but they're the next biggest fish:

| Target | nsys13 cost | What it'd cost in dev time |
|---|---:|---|
| `convert_type_kernel<bfloat16_t, float>` | **1,982 ms (8,960 calls)** | Autocast cast-elimination — eat bf16 inputs natively in more kernels |
| `Kernel2` (cutlass GEMM family) | 1,702 ms | These are already cuBLAS-selected, can't reduce |
| Cross-entropy + LM head fusion (FlashCE) | combined ~3 GB lm_head + 800 ms CE | Big custom kernel, but proven 2-3× speedup in literature |

**The convert_type_kernel is the single biggest line item in your entire kernel time**. 1,982 ms across 10 steps = ~200 ms per step. That's 22% of your total kernel time, more than ALL the matmuls combined. If you can kill half of those conversions by extending bf16 acceptance through more elementwise ops, that's a ~100 ms/step (or ~11%) throughput gain — bigger than what we just got from EPILOGUE_BIAS.

## Concrete recommendation order

1. **EPILOGUE_GELU_BIAS** for MLP fc1 — same pattern as what we just did, ~6-8 ms/step
2. **Heuristic caching** — small but trivial code change, ~1 ms/step
3. **convert_type_kernel elimination** — biggest fish, but it's an autocast-side change not matmul-side

**You were right to push back — I was about to modify the wrong file.** The actual flow:

`nn::Linear` → `autograd::addmm` → graph attaches **`AddmmBackward`** (in `MatrixBackward.cpp`), **not** `LinearBackward`.

`LinearBackward.cpp` and its dedicated `cuda_linear_bias_backward` (which calls `reduce_bias_kernel_bf16`) — **never invoked** in this codebase for GPT-2. The 0 calls of `reduce_bias_kernel_bf16` in both nsys12 and nsys13 confirm it.

What's actually computing grad_bias is [MatrixBackward.cpp:169](BluTrain_bf16_rev/Tensor-Implementations/src/autograd/backward/MatrixBackward.cpp#L169):
```cpp
grad_input = reduce_to_shape(grad_output, saved_input_shape_);
```

`reduce_to_shape` goes through the **reduction module** → `unified_reduce_kernel<__nv_bfloat16, __nv_bfloat16, SumOp<__nv_bfloat16>>`.

From the sqlite:
| Kernel | nsys13 calls | nsys13 total | nsys13 avg |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| `unified_reduce_kernel` bf16 SumOp | **240** | **20.9 ms** | 87 µs |
| `unified_reduce_kernel` fp32 SumOp | 800 | 69.6 ms | 87 µs |
| `reduce_bias_kernel_bf16` | **0** | 0 | — |
| `sum_reduction_kernel` | 240 | 0.5 ms | 2 µs |

The bf16 line is what we'd replace with BGRADA: **~20 ms / 10 steps = ~2 ms/step savings** (smaller than I initially estimated).

Note: 240 calls = 3 layers × 8 micros × 10 steps × 1 bias-grad per layer. Many of the 1,440 addmm calls likely have grad reductions in fp32 (via autocast back to master-weight dtype) — those show up in the 800-call fp32 sum bucket, not bf16.

# Full breakdown of nsys14 (and what just got fixed)

## Step-time comparison

| Step | nsys13 (ms) | nsys14 (ms) | Δ (ms) | nsys13 tok/s | nsys14 tok/s |
|------|-----------:|-----------:|------:|-------------:|-------------:|
| 1 | 878.50 | 893.70 | **+15.20** | 74,599 | 73,331 |
| 2 | 884.83 | 901.58 | **+16.75** | 74,066 | 72,690 |
| 3 | 879.65 | 909.14 | **+29.49** | 74,502 | 72,085 |
| 4 | 879.94 | 905.59 | +25.65 | 74,478 | 72,368 |
| 5 | 880.52 | 901.11 | +20.59 | 74,428 | 72,728 |
| 6 | 897.10 | 902.24 | +5.14 | 73,052 | 72,637 |
| 7 | 891.85 | 901.91 | +10.06 | 73,483 | 72,663 |
| 8 | 885.67 | 897.96 | +12.29 | 73,996 | 72,983 |
| **avg 1-8** | **884.8** | **901.7** | **+16.9** | **74,075** | **72,686** |

**~17 ms/step REGRESSION** (~1,400 tok/sec lost). Loss still matches: 9.110543 vs BluBLAS 9.110462 — math is correct, but perf is worse.

## What actually went wrong

| Kernel | nsys13 calls | nsys14 calls | Δ |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| `bf16 unified_reduce SumOp` | 240 | **240** (UNCHANGED) | 0 |
| `s1688gemm_128x128_ldg8_f2f_stages_32x1_nt` (grad_A) | 480 | **720** | **+240** |
| `s1688gemm_128x128_ldg8_f2f_tn` (grad_B) | 480 | **720** | **+240** |
| All `bgrad`/`bias`-named kernels | 0 | **0** | 0 |

Two confirmations:
1. **BGRADA never actually fired** — `bf16 unified_reduce` stayed at 240, no kernel with `bgrad` in the name appeared. cuBLASLt's heuristic rejected the BGRADA+bf16-bias config.
2. **My MatrixBackward logic ran the backward matmul twice** for those 240 eligible calls — once inside `cuda_matmul_backward_with_bias_grad` (which falls back to legacy `cublasGemmEx` when BGRADA fails) and again via my `if (!grad_input_fused) cuda_matmul_backward(...)` fallback path. 

That's the +480 extra GEMM launches = ~+170 ms / 10 steps = ~+17 ms/step. Exactly the regression we measured.

## Fix applied

Changed [MatrixBackward.cpp:257-273](BluTrain_bf16_rev/Tensor-Implementations/src/autograd/backward/MatrixBackward.cpp#L257-L273) so that when `_with_bias_grad` is called, we never call the plain `cuda_matmul_backward` again — only fall back to `reduce_to_shape` for the bias itself if BGRADA was rejected:

```cpp
if (bias_fast_path_eligible) {
    grad_input_fused = cuda_matmul_backward_with_bias_grad(...);  // does grad_A + grad_B always
    if (!grad_input_fused && beta_ != 0.0f) {
        grad_input = reduce_to_shape(grad_output, saved_input_shape_);  // only bias fallback
    }
} else {
    cuda_matmul_backward(...);  // not eligible: legacy path
}
```

## With this fix you'd get back to nsys13 level (~884 ms/step)

But BGRADA still won't fire, so there's no actual gain. The optimization is dead code unless we figure out why cuBLASLt rejects it.

## Why BGRADA likely rejected

Most-likely cause: cuBLASLt for sm_86 + `COMPUTE_32F` + bf16 GEMM + `EPILOGUE_BGRADA` requires the **bias output to be fp32**, not bf16. We set `CUBLASLT_MATMUL_DESC_BIAS_DATA_TYPE = CUDA_R_16BF` because our grad_bias is bf16.

PyTorch's analogous code uses fp32 bias output for backward bias-gradient through cuBLASLt — they then either accumulate directly into fp32 master grads (no extra convert needed) or do a tiny convert kernel of size N (cheap).

## Two options:

**Option 1: Keep the fix, verify back-to-baseline, then drop the BGRADA attempt.**
- Removes the dead-code attempt
- Returns to nsys13 perf (~884 ms/step, 74K tok/s)
- 5 minutes of work to revert
- We get nothing from the BGRADA work, but no regression either

**Option 2: Keep the fix, then retry BGRADA with fp32 bias output + tiny convert kernel.**
- ~30 min of work
- If it works: save ~21 ms / 10 steps = ~2 ms/step (+200 tok/s)
- If cuBLASLt still rejects: same as Option 1

bf16_rev **has more optimizations**: VEC=8 broadcast_scale (4× wider per-thread loads when the EPILOGUE_BIAS fast path falls through) + caching-allocator integration.

what are those g_lt stats?do we need those aha ?

### CLAUDE [2026-06-13 20:31 IST]

Plain answer: **it's a debug-only counter system you almost certainly don't need.**

## What `g_lt_stats` actually is

A small struct of atomic counters that tracks **why the cuBLASLt fast path fell through** on each `cuda_addmm` call. Counters:

```cpp
struct CublasLtAddmmStats {
    std::atomic<uint64_t> entered;                // how many addmm calls came in
    std::atomic<uint64_t> succeeded;              // how many actually fused via cuBLASLt
    std::atomic<uint64_t> fail_out_noncontig;     // ↓ reason buckets ↓
    std::atomic<uint64_t> fail_a_layout;
    std::atomic<uint64_t> fail_b_layout;
    std::atomic<uint64_t> fail_batch_dims;
    std::atomic<uint64_t> fail_unsupported_dtype;
    std::atomic<uint64_t> heuristic_empty;
    std::atomic<uint64_t> matmul_failed;
    std::atomic<int>      last_status;
};
```

At program exit (only if you ran with `CUBLASLT_DEBUG=1` env var), it prints something like:
```
[CUBLASLT_DEBUG] cuda_addmm fast-path stats:
   entered=1440  succeeded=1440
   fail_out_noncontig=0  fail_a_layout=0  fail_b_layout=0
   fail_batch_dims=0  fail_unsupported_dtype=0
   heuristic_empty=0  matmul_failed=0  last_status=0
```

So you can answer "why didn't my fast path fire?" without a profiler.

## Do you need it?

**Probably no.** We already have all this info from nsys:
- If we see `ampere_..._relu_f2f` kernel calls → fast path fired
- If we see `broadcast_scale_kernel` calls → fast path fell through to legacy
- nsys13/14/15 already showed fast path firing 1440/1440 times (100%)

So nsys gives you the "did it fire?" answer for free. The g_lt_stats system only helps when:
1. You're developing a new shape/dtype path and want quick CLI feedback without running nsys
2. You want categorized failure reasons (whereas nsys just shows the resulting kernel)

**Cost**: ~8 atomic fetch_add() per call = a few nanoseconds. Negligible perf impact.

## My recommendation

**Skip the counters.** Just copy `bf16_rev`'s `GenMatmul.cu` verbatim to the other 3 folders. Reasons:
- We already use nsys to verify hit rates → don't need the env-var print
- One less thing to merge / maintain
- 5 min of work vs 30 min
- Cleaner result file (no atomic counter sprinkled through `launch_cublaslt_addmm`)

If you ever need to debug a fast-path miss, you can grep nsys output for `broadcast_scale_kernel` calls. Same info.

Want me to proceed with the simple copy? Or do you have a specific reason to want the counters in the production path?

### USER [2026-06-13 20:31 IST]

does hat affect matmul speed aah ?

### CLAUDE [2026-06-13 20:31 IST]

**No — zero measurable effect on matmul speed.** Here's the math:

The counters are `std::atomic<uint64_t>` with `fetch_add(1, std::memory_order_relaxed)` — the cheapest possible atomic. Each one is ~5-10 ns on x86 (one `LOCK XADD` instruction).

Per `cuda_addmm` call: 2 atomic increments (one on entry, one on success/fail) = ~10-20 ns.

Total cost across our entire training run:
- 1,440 addmm calls × 20 ns = **28.8 µs**
- Spread across 10 steps = **~3 µs per step**
- That's **0.0003%** of the ~880 ms step time

Three more reasons it doesn't matter:
1. The increments happen **on the CPU**, before the kernel launch — the GPU never sees them
2. The matmul kernel itself runs hundreds of µs and is completely unaffected
3. We never measure anything down to the µs level — our run-to-run noise is already ±5 ms/step

So the counters are basically free perf-wise. The only reason to skip them is **maintenance burden** — extra code to keep, extra failure paths to update if we ever add a new fast-path branch.

## Bottom line

The choice between "with counters" vs "without counters" is **purely about code complexity, not performance**.

If you want maximum diagnostic visibility for future debugging → keep them.
If you want minimum cruft → skip them.

What's your preference?

**Author:** Goutham Reddy (Gautam_1926)

restructure, and bug-fix shipped across the framework — folding together the
earlier `SPRINT_OPTIMIZATIONS_FULL_RECORD.md` (16 sprint items), the per-topic
docs in `master_gau_latest_ada_6000_sm89/docs/`, and the **post-sprint work**

| Tag | Path | Role |

## Part A — The sprint optimizations (16 items)

Source of truth: `master_gau_latest_ada_6000_sm89/docs/SPRINT_OPTIMIZATIONS_FULL_RECORD.md`.
The sprint had two phases: **kernel-centric** (open a kernel, fix GPU idle/waste)
then **training-centric** (eliminate cross-kernel redundancy visible only from
the training-loop lens). Condensed below; all verified **byte-identical in B**
(md5-compared) unless noted.

### Kernel-centric phase

1. **Reductions module overhaul** (previous sprint's foundation) ✅
   - Replaced 4 copy-pasted kernels (~800 lines) with one `unified_reduce_kernel`
     (functor `ops_t`). PyTorch-style 3-path layout dispatch
     (`InnerContiguous` / `OuterContiguous` / `Generic`).
   - Multi-stage launch dispatcher (`nt ∈ {32,64,128,256,512}`), cascade (pairwise)
     sum for FP precision, Welford one-pass variance, O(1) `reduced_bitmap`
     replacing 22× `std::find`, packed `__grid_constant__` metadata, caching
     allocator for intermediates, NaN-deterministic argmin/argmax.
   - Files: `ReductionKernels.cuh`, `ReductionImpl.h`, `ReductionUtils.h`,
     `ReductionOps.h`, `ReductionImplGPU.cu`.

2. **GeLU full restructure (fwd+bwd, CPU+GPU)** ✅
   - Adopted the uniform **3-layer pattern**: `nn::GeLU.forward()` →
     `autograd::gelu()` (thin wrapper, saves tensor + attaches grad node) →
     `gelu_forward()` (pure math: CPU AVX2 or GPU dispatch). **Math lives OUTSIDE
     the autograd wrapper.** `ActivationOps.cpp` 400→179 lines;
     `ActivationBackward.cpp` 277→90 lines.
   - GPU: `fused_gelu_kernel_vectorized` (`float4` + `fast_tanh`); ReLU rewritten
     branch-free `(x+|x|)*0.5`; `fused_bias_gelu` templated for fp16/bf16.
   - Deleted buggy legacy `include/mlp/`, `src/mlp-blocks/`.

3. **Activation module restructure (ReLU, Dropout, Softmax, RMSNorm)** ✅
   - Same 3-layer pattern across the suite. RMSNorm via PyTorch's
     `bool rms_norm` template-parameter trick (same LayerNorm kernel reused with
     `if constexpr`, zero runtime cost).

4. **LayerNorm restructure + RMSNorm** ✅
   - CPU fwd 3→2 passes (Welford) + AVX2 + FMA; CPU bwd OMP + thread-local + AVX2;
     GPU `bool rms_norm` template fwd, `float4`-vectorized bwd; `__launch_bounds__(512)`
     on sm89. RTX 3060 numbers: CPU fwd 12.3×, CPU bwd 5.6×, GPU bwd ~20% faster.

5. **Memset cleanup — sync→async + redundant removal** ✅
   - All blocking `cudaMemset` → `cudaMemsetAsync(...,stream)`; removed memsets on
     buffers the next kernel fully overwrites (LN bwd ~25K memsets, attn dQ
     `zeros`→`empty` ~4K). Saved ~3.2 s/run.

6. **Stride-aware attention kernels** ✅
   - Kernels accept per-tensor strides; removed pre-kernel `.contiguous()` on
     Q/K/V/grad_out (~17K kernels, ~2 s).

7. **Contiguous kernel — 4-path hybrid dispatcher** ✅
   - `coalesce_dimensions` → (3a) DMA `cudaMemcpyAsync` for fully-contiguous,
     (3b) tiled shared-mem 2D transpose, (3c) vectorized `float4`/`uint2` inner
     copy, (3d) generic FastDivmod fallback. `ContiguousMeta` via
     `__grid_constant__`. FastDivmod ≈ 6× vs `%`/`/`. (A later Path-3d add covered
     `[B,S,H,D]→[B,H,S,D]`.)

8. **BatchedCat fused kernel** ✅
   - PyTorch-style `CatArrayBatchedCopy` — one launch for an N-way concat instead
     of N `cudaMemcpy2DAsync`. (`misc/BatchedCat.cu`.)

### Lens shift (§9) → training-centric phase

1. **Perspective shift** — from "is this kernel optimal?" to "is this kernel's I/O
   necessary given what the producer wrote and the consumer wants?"

2. **`Tensor::contiguous()` short-circuit** ✅
    - Was allocating + copying even when the input was already contiguous. Fix
      (`Tensor.cpp:283-287`):

      ```cpp
      if (is_contiguous() && storage_offset() == 0) { return *this; }
      ```

      Returns a view, not a copy.

3. **`multi_tensor_zero_grad` kernel + factory rewrites** ✅ (see Part C #1 for the
    Optim.cpp call-site, which needed re-enabling in B)
    - `multi_tensor_zero_sm89_kernel` zeros up to N tensors per launch (uint4 /
      STG.128 16-byte stores, 128 KB chunks, `block_to_tensor`/`block_to_chunk`
      metadata). `fill_cuda_launch<T>` (vec16/vec8/scalar) for ones/full.
    - Factory: `Tensor::zeros` → `cudaMemsetAsync` (no CPU round-trip);
      `ones`/`full` → `fill_cuda_launch`; bool byte-memset fix.

4. **CachingCudaAllocator integration** ✅
    - `cudaMallocAsync`-backed caching allocator on all compute allocations;
      removed two `cudaStreamSynchronize` from the free path (event-based reuse).

5. **Sparse cross-entropy save_max/save_sum** ✅
    - Forward saves `saved_max[B]`/`saved_sum[B]`; backward reads logits once
      (3×→2× the 3.3 GB logit traffic). ~half PyTorch's memory traffic.

6. **Matmul: cuBLASLt port + fused-bias epilogue** ✅ (see Part C / Part B for B status)
    - `CUBLAS_COMPUTE_32F_FAST_TF32` via `cublasLtMatmulAlgoGetHeuristic` →
      `cublasLtMatmul`; `CUBLASLT_EPILOGUE_BIAS` fuses bias into the GEMM
      (`broadcast_scale_kernel` ~17,280→1 per run). Backward DGELU_BGRAD fusion
      still **deferred**.

7. **Smart reshape (`compute_view_stride`)** ✅
    - Mirrors PyTorch `computeStride_impl`; view-eligible reshapes skip
      `.contiguous()` (killed 12,960 strided copies/run).

8. **Packed SDPA** ✅
    - Q/K/V read via strided pointer offsets into packed `[B,T,3C]`; output written
      as packed `[B,T,C]` (the layout `c_proj` wants) — eliminates the
      post-attention merge copy. `skip_grad_zero` flag for the packed backward.
      Env-gated (`USE_PACKED_SDPA=1`). Killed 15,840 generic + 3,840 cat copies.

**Sprint wall-time roll-up:** baseline 126.56 s, PyTorch 113.04 s; cuBLASLt port
(~10 s) + Packed SDPA (~3 s) + memset/stride/reshape close the gap and pass
PyTorch in some runs.

## Part B — Correctness fixes (cross-cutting)

- **Weight-tying scramble bug (permanent fix).** ✅ in B.
  Root cause: `TransposeBackward` returned a strided view →
  `AutogradMeta::accumulate_grad` adopted it as-is → stride-blind multi-tensor
  Adam walked `param[i]`/`grad[i]` flat → deterministic per-element permutation
  of `wte.weight` grad. Fix = **Gradient Layout Contract** in `AutogradMeta.cpp`
  (mirrors PyTorch `AccumulateGrad` Case 1.5: materialize a contiguous clone if
  the incoming grad doesn't obey the contract). `TransposeBackward.cpp` reverted
  to the simple form (v3 — the contract handles it generically).
  Verification metrics: wte cos 0.001→0.99999994 after fix.
  *(`AutogradMeta.cpp`, `TransposeBackward.cpp` byte-identical in B.)*
  Doc: `weight_tying_bug_fix.md`.

- **Reductions — the 4 bugs + extras.** ✅ in B.
  (1) CPU middle-axis demotion (`cpu_demote_middle_to_generic`), (2) InnerContiguous
  GPU, (3) GPU non-consecutive multi-axis (`setup_generic_path`), (4) vec4/multi-CTA
  `block_y_reduce` missing trailing `__syncthreads()`. Plus **NaN-check removal in
  `reduce_add`/`reduce_mul`** (`ReductionOps.h` — IEEE add/mul already propagate
  NaN, so the branch was redundant), **multi-CTA staging**, **vec4 output**, and
  **fastdivmod** (Granlund–Montgomery division-by-invariant) in the generic path.
  Also a **32-bit indexing guard** (`ensure_32bit_reduction_indexing`): GPU reduce
  indexes with uint32 + int counts → throws for numel > 2³²−1 or counts > 2³¹−1
  (PyTorch-style split is the deferred real fix). Validated **90/90** on sm_86.
  Docs: `reduction_bias_gradient_bug_fix.md`, `data_accessing_techniques.md`.

- **Adam `eps` placement (multi-tensor kernel).** ✅ in B.
  `denom = sqrtf(v_hat) + eps` — eps **inside** the denominator (not the old
  `rsqrtf(v_hat) + eps` which gave `rsqrtf(0)=+Inf → NaN` at v=0). Present in both
  the inline and macro forms (`MultiTensorKernels_sm89.cu`).

- **Types.h fp32→bf16 inf-clamp bug.** 🆕 fixed in B this session — see Part C #5.

## Part C — Post-sprint work (2026-06-08): BluTrain → B sync + gap-closing ports

Method: full `diff -rq` of parked **BluTrain** vs **B** → only **25 files** differ;
everything else byte-identical (so B already had the whole sprint). Triaged the 25
(token-normalized to strip clang-format noise) and closed the real gaps. Each port
built clean (`make tensor`, exit 0) on sm_86.

### 1. `multi_tensor_zero_grad` re-enabled in B's `Optimizer::zero_grad()` 🆕

- **Before (B):** `zero_grad()` looped `for (p:params) p.zero_grad();` — **one
  tensor zeroed at a time** (148 separate ops for GPT-2's 148 params). Scalar path.
- **After:** collects all GPU-resident grads into `std::vector<cuda::ZeroTensorInfo>`
  and calls `cuda::multi_tensor_zero_cuda(gpu_grads)` → `multi_tensor_zero_sm89_cuda`
  → **one batched kernel** zeroing many tensors/launch (uint4 STG.128, 128 KB
  chunks). ~4 launches instead of 148. Dtype-agnostic (bit-pattern 0 = value 0).
  CPU grads keep the per-param path.
- File: `src/nn/optimizer/Optim.cpp` (`zero_grad` body). The `multi_tensor_zero_*`
  kernel + wrapper already existed in B; only the call-site was the slow loop.
- ⚠️ **Validate with one training step** (only behavioral change in this batch).

### 2. RELU branch-free math 🆕

- `src/Kernels/cuda/activations/RELUKernels.cu`.
- fwd: `val > 0 ? val : 0` → **`(val + fabsf(val)) * 0.5f`** (the "x + |x| over 2"
  form; propagates NaN, unlike the ternary).
- bwd: `val > 0 ? grad : 0` → `grad * (float)(val > 0)` (branch-free mask).

### 3. GELU fused-bias epilogue — fp16/bf16 templated 🆕

- `src/Kernels/cuda/activations/GELUKernels.cu`.
- B previously had **float-only** `fused_bias_gelu` (fwd + bwd). Now **templated**
  for `float`/`__half`/`__nv_bfloat16` (storage type T, math in fp32 via
  `to_float`/`from_float`); added `launch_fused_bias_gelu` and
  `launch_fused_bias_gelu_backward` overloads for half/bf16 (grad_bias stays fp32).
  Float entry points unchanged → ABI-safe, no caller breaks.

### 4. LayerNorm/RMSNorm backward `cudaMemset` → `cudaMemsetAsync` 🆕

- `src/Kernels/cuda/norm/LayerNormKernels.cu` — 3 sites
  (`launch_layer_norm_backward` grad_gamma/grad_beta; `launch_rms_norm_backward` gg)
  switched from blocking `cudaMemset` to `cudaMemsetAsync(...,0)` so they don't
  serialize the default stream.

### 5. Types.h fp32→bf16 inf-clamp **bug** removed 🆕

- `include/dtype/Types.h`, `float_to_bfloat16`.
- **Bug:** an extra `if (exponent > 0x8E) return Inf;` clamped every `|x| ≥ 2¹⁶`
  (=65536) to infinity. But bf16 shares fp32's 8-bit exponent → its range is
  ~3.4e38; nothing in that range overflows. The clamp was an **fp16-style guard
  mis-applied to bf16** and wrongly flushed normal values ≥ 65536 to Inf.
- **Fix:** removed the clamp (bf16 needs only the `exponent == 0xFF` NaN/Inf
  passthrough). `float_to_float16` (fp16, 5-bit exponent, max ~65504) legitimately
  keeps its overflow handling and was left untouched.
- Latent for current runs (GPT-2 trains fp32), real for any bf16 path.

### 6. TensorImpl.cpp `zero_grad` dtype dispatch 🆕 (ported earlier this session)

- `src/core/TensorImpl.cpp`. Replaced `grad_tensor.fill<float>(0.0f)` (threw
  "Fill: Datatype mismatch" for fp16/bf16 grads on the CPU path) with
  `dispatch_by_dtype(...).fill<T>(static_cast<T>(0))`. Mirrors the multi-tensor
  byte-memset's dtype-agnostic behavior.

### 7. cuBLASLt fused-bias matmul (ported A → B earlier this session) ✅

- `src/Kernels/cuda/matmul/GenMatmul.cu` — the `CUBLASLT_EPILOGUE_BIAS` fused-bias
  path (A's inline version) injected into B's `#else` (default-cublas) branch of
  `#ifdef WITH_MYBLAS`. Pure addition (+247 lines), no other change.
  *(Note: `master_gau`'s `CHANGES_AND_FIXES_LOG.md` documents a separate
  `CublasLtHelper.cu`; B uses the inline-in-GenMatmul approach — functionally the
  fused-bias path is present either way.)*

## Part D — TF32 rounding: round-half-up (`+0x1000u`) vs RNE (analysis)

In the attention kernels (`AttentionForward.cu`, `AttentionForward_sm89.cu`,
`AttentionBackward.cu`, `AttentionBackward_sm89.cu`) and the SM89 matmul core, the
fp32→TF32 operand conversion uses a **biased round-half-up**: `reg += 0x1000u`
before the `mma.sync.tf32`. (BluTrain switched its attention kernels to RNE; **B
keeps `+0x1000u`** — a deliberate perf choice, documented here as intentional.)

**What `+0x1000u` is:** TF32 keeps the top 19 bits of fp32 (drops the low 13
mantissa bits). Adding `0x1000` = half of the 2¹³ truncation unit, then letting the
MMA drop the low 13 bits = **round-to-nearest with ties rounding up** ("round
half up", a.k.a. round-half-toward-+∞ in bit space ≈ round-half-away-from-zero for
positives). Without the bias, truncation alone is round-toward-zero (biased down).

**RNE (round-half-to-even)** uses a value-dependent bias:
`lsb = (r>>13)&1; r = (r + 0x0FFFu + lsb) & 0xFFFFE000u`. Ties round to the even
LSB — bit-exactly matching cuBLAS.

| | round-half-up `+0x1000u` | RNE |
|---|---|---|
| Instructions / element | **1** (`IADD`) | **~4–5** (shift, and, add, add, and) |
| When result differs | — | **only at the exact tie** (discarded bits == `0x1000`) |
| Matches cuBLAS at ties | no (tiny upward bias) | yes |

**Answers to the recurring questions:**

- *"Do the two differ only at 0.5?"* — **Yes.** At every non-tie value both round
  to the same nearest TF32; they disagree **only** at the exact midpoint.
- *"With RNE, do instructions increase only at 0.5, or always?"* — **Always.** RNE
  is *branchless* straight-line code; it runs the same ~4–5 ops for every element,
  tie or not. The instruction count is constant (and constantly higher than 1).
  What's tie-specific is the **result**, not the op count.
- *"Can RNE be 1 instruction?"* — **No.** Round-half-up uses a *constant* bias
  (`0x1000`) → single add. RNE's bias depends on the value's own bit-13 (to break
  ties to even), so it must be *computed* first → inherently ≥ 3–4 ops.
- **Conclusion:** ties are astronomically rare in real float products, so
  `+0x1000u` gives effectively identical numerics at 1 op vs ~4–5 — **strictly
  faster, same results in practice.** Keeping `+0x1000u` in B is correct for a
  throughput build. The only caveat: not bit-identical to cuBLAS at exact ties
  (a strict bit-parity test could show a handful of 1-ULP-TF32 diffs).

> Context: this replaces an earlier broken **"3×TF32"** path (which is why the
> SM89 GEMM comment still says "3xTF32 emulation" though the code now does a
> single TF32 MMA + rounding bias). True 3×TF32 would split each fp32 into 3 TF32
> limbs and issue 3–6 MMAs for near-fp32 accuracy; the single-MMA + round-half-up
> is the perf-favoring choice.

## Part F — Post-sprint work (2026-06-11): 16-bit input-vectorization in the reduction kernel

**Repo:** `BluTrain_best_precision` (BBP). **GPU tested:** RTX 3060 (sm_86), CUDA 13.0.

**What & why.** Ported PyTorch's *"vectorize along input"* path (`Reduce.cuh`
`input_vectorized_thread_reduce_impl`) for **16-bit** dtypes on the
**InnerContiguous** (last-axis) reduction. Previously the kernel used scalar
strided loads with `VT0 = 4` accumulators for *all* dtypes/paths. Now, for fp16/bf16
InnerContiguous reductions, a thread issues **one 128-bit `VecLoad<scalar_t,8>`** per
step into **8 independent accumulators** — matching PyTorch's `input_vec_size = 8`
for 2-byte types (eight 16-bit values fill one 128-bit transaction).

**Files changed** (`Tensor-Implementations/`):

- `include/ops/helpers/ReductionKernels.cuh`:
  - `GpuReduceConfig`: new `int input_vec_size = 1` field.
  - `build_reduce_config<acc_t>(…, int input_vec_hint = 1)`: gate that sets
    `input_vec_size` and divides the block work-dim by the width. Fires only for
    `path == InnerContiguous && reduced_count >= 128 && reduced_count % hint == 0`.
    `num_inputs` stays in **element** units (so `MeanOps`' divisor and the multi-CTA
    math are unchanged); only the block geometry is sized in vector units.
  - `unified_reduce_kernel<…, int VT0, bool INPUT_VEC>`: new `INPUT_VEC` branch in
    the InnerContiguous case doing the contiguous 128-bit load into `acc[VT0]`.
  - Launcher: dispatches the `VT0=8, INPUT_VEC=true` kernel under
    `if constexpr (sizeof(scalar_t) == 2) { if (config.input_vec_size == 8) … }`,
    so the 8-wide kernel is only instantiated for 2-byte types.
- `src/UnaryOps/cuda/ReductionImplGPU.cu`: value-reduction and mean dispatchers pass
  `input_vec_hint = (sizeof(CudaT)==2) ? 8 : 1`. Index (argmin/argmax) and Welford
  variance dispatchers keep `hint = 1` (scalar path) — index ops need the per-element
  index, Welford is precision-sensitive.

**Design choices (honest scope):**

- **InnerContiguous only.** OuterContiguous (the bias-gradient case) reduces a
  *strided* axis, so a thread can't pull 8 contiguous values in one load — it stays
  on the `VT0=4` strided path (unchanged). fp32 also stays on `VT0=4` everywhere.
- **No head/tail peel.** Gating on `reduced_count % 8 == 0` guarantees every row base
  is 16-byte aligned, so the wide load is always aligned — simpler than PyTorch's
  unaligned-head handling. Non-multiples of 8 fall back to the scalar path.
- 4-accumulator ILP remains the **baseline for all paths**; 8 is the **only** 16-bit
  InnerContiguous exception.

**Validation.** `reduction_final_test` = **112/112** (90 prior + 22 new input-vec
cases: gate-on at 128/256/1024/2048/1000, gate-off fallbacks at 1001/130/<128,
multi-CTA at 262144, mean-divisor checks; f16 + bf16). New micro-benchmark
`reduction_ilp_benchmark.cpp` for before/after timings.

**Measured (RTX 3060, `sum(axis=-1)`, before → after):**

| case | f16 / bf16 | note |
|---|---|---|
| aligned large (e.g. 16384×1024) | +2–6% | already ~92% of ~360 GB/s — bandwidth-bound |
| `R = 1000` (= 8×125) | **~+25%** (241 → 300 GB/s) | scalar strided path was inefficient here |
| fp32, and non-÷8 sizes | unchanged | correctly stay on scalar path |

No regressions; the expected register-pressure occupancy drop from 8 accumulators did
not materialise.

**Caveats / not done:** small win on the 3060 because 16-bit reductions are already
bandwidth-bound there — likely larger on higher-bandwidth GPUs (A100/H100/B300),
**not yet benchmarked** on those. The formal technical report (`madhu.txt`/`.pdf`) was
**left unchanged**: its ILP paragraph + benchmarks are scoped to the bias-gradient
(OuterContiguous) path, which this change does not touch, and we have no
report-hardware (RTX 6000 Ada) numbers for the InnerContiguous path. Full conceptual
write-up (4-vs-8, Volkov/latency truth + sources) is in
`bias_gradient_reduce_sum_kernel_usage.md` §10.3.

## Part G — Post-sprint work (2026-06-13): `BluTrain_bf16_rev` GenMatmul sweep + merged file across 4 BluTrain folders

Working repo: `BluTrain_bf16_rev/` (the BF16 mixed-precision training branch).
Goal: optimize the cuBLAS-backend `GenMatmul.cu` for bf16/fp16 workloads, then
roll the merged result back to all 4 BluTrain folders.

### G.1. Dtype-mismatch bug (latent in all branches) — **fixed**

The `dispatch_by_dtype` system hands `bfloat16_t` / `float16_t` (project wrapper
structs in `include/dtype/Types.h`, each just a `uint16_t raw_bits` field) to
template-instantiated kernels. Multiple predicates in `GenMatmul.cu` only checked
the NATIVE CUDA types:

```cpp
if constexpr (std::is_same_v<T, __nv_bfloat16>) { ... }   // never fires for dispatch
```

Result: vectorized branches silently fell through to scalar / zero-init fallbacks
when the input was `bfloat16_t`/`float16_t` — a correctness-adjacent perf bug
present in **all** branches (`bf16_rev`, `best_precision`, `BluTrain_old`,
`may27_406k`). **7 sites fixed** to check BOTH wrapper and native types:

- `cublaslt_dtype<T>()` — fp16 + bf16 mapping (2 sites)
- `broadcast_scale_kernel<T>` — fp16 + bf16 vectorized branches (2 sites)
- `launch_broadcast_scale<T>` VEC selector (1 site)
- `add_scaled_kernel_typed` (1 site)
- `cuda_addmm` cuBLASLt dispatch lambda (1 site)

The merged file ships the fix to all 4 branches simultaneously.

### G.2. `broadcast_scale_kernel` VEC=2 → VEC=8 (fp16/bf16 paths)

Old code: VEC=2 for fp16/bf16 → 32-bit per-thread loads (one `__nv_bfloat162`
or `__half2`). Pushed to VEC=8 → 128-bit per-thread loads via `float4`
reinterpretation (4 × pair). Matches PyTorch's `can_vectorize_up_to<scalar_t>()`
(`pytorch_source/aten/src/ATen/native/cuda/MemoryAccess.cuh:508-533`) which
returns 8 for fp16/bf16 when pointer-aligned.

VEC selector at launcher:

```cpp
constexpr int VEC =
    std::is_same_v<T, float> ? 4
    : (std::is_same_v<T, __half> || std::is_same_v<T, float16_t> ||
       std::is_same_v<T, __nv_bfloat16> || std::is_same_v<T, bfloat16_t>) ? 8
    : std::is_same_v<T, double> ? 2 : 1;
```

**Measured** (`nsys12` VEC=8 vs `nsys9` VEC=2, both with EPILOGUE_BIAS OFF):

- `broadcast_scale_kernel` per-call avg: 68 µs → **44 µs** (−35%)
- Grid sizes 4× smaller (1536 / 4608 / 6144 vs 6144 / 18432 / 24576)
- Step 9 loss unchanged: 9.110535 (matches BluBLAS reference 9.110462)

Note: when EPILOGUE_BIAS is on (Part G.3), `broadcast_scale_kernel` is dead code
(never called) — the VEC=8 work is defensive for shapes where cuBLASLt's
heuristic might decline.

### G.3. cuBLASLt `EPILOGUE_BIAS` workspace — raw `cudaMalloc` → `AllocatorRegistry::get_caching_allocator()`

Was: per-device raw `cudaMalloc(32 MiB)` for the cuBLASLt workspace, leaked at
process exit. Switched to the project's caching allocator (the same one used by
`ReductionKernels.cuh` scratch buffers). Workspace allocated lazily on the first
`cuda_addmm` call per device.

### G.4. `nsys13` results (final EPILOGUE_BIAS + VEC=8 + dtype fixes)

GPT-2-44M BF16 autocast, B=8, T=1024, 10 steps, 3 transformer layers,
weight-tying OFF, sm_86 (RTX 3060):

| Metric | nsys12 (VEC=8 only) | **nsys13 (+ EPILOGUE_BIAS)** | Δ |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Avg step time (steps 1-8) | 891.5 ms | **884.8 ms** | −6.7 ms |
| Avg throughput | 73,514 tok/s | **74,075 tok/s** | +561 |
| Step 9 loss | 9.110535 | 9.110526 | ~0 (matches BluBLAS 9.110462) |
| `broadcast_scale_kernel` calls | 1,440 (62.25 ms) | **0** | −1,440 / −62.25 ms |
| Legacy `s1688gemm` addmm calls | 1,440 (436 ms) | **0** | −1,440 / −436 ms |
| Fused `s16816gemm_*_relu_f2f_*` calls | 0 | 1,440 (478 ms) | new fused-bias path |
| `cudaMemset` count | 4,986 | 3,546 | −1,440 |
| `cudaMemset` total bytes | 27.3 GB | **7.9 GB** | −19.4 GB |
| Total GPU kernel launches | 28,839 | 27,399 | −1,440 |

Fast path hit rate: **1,440 / 1,440 (100%)**. cuBLASLt's heuristic also picked
**larger tile widths** (`s16816gemm_256x128_32x3_nn` instead of
`s1688gemm_64x128_sliced1x2`) — a side benefit of the heuristic having access
to the bias as a fused epilogue op.

### G.5. Failed BGRADA backward bias-grad fusion (reverted)

Attempted `CUBLASLT_EPILOGUE_BGRADA` to fuse
`grad_bias = sum_i grad_output[i, :]` into the `grad_weight` backward matmul
(`A^T @ grad_output`).

**Important finding about the bias-grad code path**: the wired-up
`LinearBackward.cpp` + `cuda_linear_bias_backward` + `reduce_bias_kernel_bf16`
are **dead code** in this repo — `reduce_bias_kernel_bf16` recorded 0 calls in
every nsys. The actual bias-grad path goes through:

```text
nn::Linear.forward() → autograd::addmm
                  → AddmmBackward::apply (MatrixBackward.cpp:155)
                  → reduce_to_shape(grad_output, saved_input_shape_)
                  → reduce_sum
                  → unified_reduce_kernel<__nv_bfloat16, SumOp<bf16>>
```

240 calls / 20.9 ms total of `unified_reduce_kernel<bf16,SumOp<bf16>>` in
nsys13 are the bias-grad reductions. So I wired the BGRADA attempt into
`AddmmBackward::apply`, not `LinearBackward.cpp`.

**Two reasons it failed**:

1. **cuBLASLt heuristic rejection on sm_86**: with `COMPUTE_32F` + bf16 GEMM +
   bf16 bias output (`CUBLASLT_MATMUL_DESC_BIAS_DATA_TYPE = CUDA_R_16BF`), the
   heuristic returns 0 algos. PyTorch's analogous backward paths use **fp32
   bias output** even for bf16 GEMMs. Likely BGRADA requires fp32 bias on this
   arch.

2. **Double-matmul bug in caller**: my `MatrixBackward.cpp` was:

   ```cpp
   grad_input_fused = cuda_matmul_backward_with_bias_grad(...);  // already did matmul + failed BGRADA
   if (!grad_input_fused) cuda_matmul_backward(...);              // did matmul AGAIN
   ```

   Each of the 240 eligible backward calls did the matmul TWICE (1 × `_nt` for
   grad_A + 1 × `_tn` for grad_B = +480 extra `s1688gemm` launches per run).
   Measured **+17 ms/step regression** in `nsys14`.

**Revert verified by `nsys15`**: 882.9 ms/step avg, 74,228 tok/s — matches the
nsys13 baseline within noise (±2 ms/step is normal run-to-run noise).

**Path forward if BGRADA is retried later**:

- Allocate `grad_bias` as **fp32** in the autograd graph
- Use a tiny N-element `fp32 → bf16` convert kernel after the BGRADA matmul
- Never let the fused-variant fallback double-run the matmul work — when the
  fused helper declines, only fall back for the BIAS computation
- Set `CUBLASLT_DEBUG=1` to see heuristic_empty count to confirm BGRADA fires

Saved as memory: `project_bgrada_bf16_unsupported.md`.

### G.6. `g_lt_stats` diagnostic counters ported from `best_precision`

`best_precision`'s `GenMatmul.cu` had granular failure-bucket counters that
`bf16_rev`'s version lacked. Ported into the merged file:

```cpp
struct CublasLtAddmmStats {
    std::atomic<uint64_t> entered, succeeded;
    std::atomic<uint64_t> fail_out_noncontig, fail_a_layout, fail_b_layout;
    std::atomic<uint64_t> fail_batch_dims, fail_unsupported_config;
    std::atomic<uint64_t> heuristic_empty, matmul_failed;
    std::atomic<cublasStatus_t> last_status;
};
```

Set env `CUBLASLT_DEBUG=1` to get an atexit dump:

```text
[CUBLASLT_DEBUG] cuda_addmm fast-path stats:
   entered=1440  succeeded=1440  fail_out_noncontig=0  fail_a_layout=0
   fail_b_layout=0  fail_batch_dims=0  fail_unsupported_config=0
   heuristic_empty=0  matmul_failed=0  last_status=0
```

CPU overhead: ~2 × `LOCK XADD` per addmm call = ~10-20 ns. Lost in run-to-run
noise (~28 µs total per training run, **0.0003%** of step time).

### G.7. Merged `GenMatmul.cu` deployed to all 4 BluTrain folders

Final merged file = `bf16_rev` (VEC=8 + caching allocator + 7 dtype fixes +
EPILOGUE_BIAS) + `best_precision` (g_lt_stats). Byte-identical across:

| Folder | Path | Pre-existing state |
|---|---|---|
| `BluTrain_bf16_rev` | `Tensor-Implementations/src/Kernels/cuda/matmul/GenMatmul.cu` | source of truth |
| `BluTrain_best_precision` | same | had EPILOGUE_BIAS + g_lt_stats, no VEC=8, no dtype fixes |
| `BluTrain_old` | same | older, had EPILOGUE_BIAS, no VEC=8, no dtype fixes, no g_lt_stats |
| `BluTrain_BestPrecisionEnv_may27_406k` | `BluTrain_BestPrecisionEnv/BluTrain/Tensor-Implementations/src/Kernels/cuda/matmul/GenMatmul.cu` | identical to `best_precision` |

`GenMatmul.o` built clean in each. `.bak.<unix_timestamp>` backups preserved in
each of the 3 overwritten locations.

**Why this matters**: the dtype-mismatch fix and VEC=8 broadcast_scale improvements
were ONLY in `bf16_rev`. The other 3 folders had:

- **Silent dtype-mismatch bug**: their `broadcast_scale_kernel` was hitting the
  scalar fallback instead of the vectorized branch whenever a `bfloat16_t` or
  `float16_t` wrapper tensor was passed (which is what `dispatch_by_dtype`
  always does in autocast mode)
- **VEC=2** broadcast_scale even when EPILOGUE_BIAS fell through (4× slower
  per-thread than the bf16_rev VEC=8 version)

Merged file fixes all 4.

### G.8. Caveat — numerical verification only done in `bf16_rev`

I built `GenMatmul.o` clean in each target repo but did NOT run training in
`BluTrain_old`, `best_precision`, or `may27_406k`. If those repos have specific
loss baselines, a sanity-training run is wise before relying on them. The
math should be identical (same numerical operations, same TF32 mode, same
accumulator dtypes) — only the broadcast_scale VEC width changed in any
visible way, and that's purely a vectorization layout change.
